margin-bottom:20px;"><img src="https://cdn.triathlete.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/TheRealGrandTheft.jpg" alt="The Real Grand Theft" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy"></div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div class="article-body" style="font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.8; color: #333;"> <p>Editor-in-chief Chris Foster on why Grand Theft Auto 6 costs more than you think (Photo: Donald Miralle/Getty Images)</p> <p>Everything has a price, and I think the cost of Grand Theft Auto 6 — the much-anticipated sixth installment of one of the most successful video games in history — is far too high.</p> <p>And I’m not talking about the cost to your wallet.</p> <p>Triathletes know all about expensive things: Tri bikes now start around $3,000 and rise (quickly) up from there; running shoes now routinely crack $200; and now we’ve got $270 swim caps. And don’t even get me started on entry fees — the 2026 Hawaii Ironman World Championship, for instance, topped $1,800, granted that’s easily our sport’s crown jewel, but even a new event like 70.3 Utah still rings in around $500.</p> <p>And even once you factor in the hard out-of-pocket expenses for equipment, entry fees, pool access, coaching, travel, accommodations, and so on, there’s still that looming cost of your time.</p> <p>If a midpack triathlete did our most popular 70.3 training plan, they’d spend about 150 hours of training over the 20-week plan. For our 16-week Olympic-distance plan, a triathlete would rack up about 65 hours of training. That’s a pretty big chunk of life, but I know I’m preaching to the choir, as all triathletes know that our sport requires a big time commitment.</p> <p>However, there’s good news: We know that much of that time can be spent outside; we know that — when done correctly — swimming, biking, and running are very healthy endeavors; and we know that much of that time can be spent with others. To that last point, for better or for worse, even just the act of going to a race forces us to participate within a community.</p> <p>In a digital world that competes ferociously for our attention (think: social media, TV, AI, and so on), giving your precious and valuable attention — your time — to swimming, biking, running, and triathlon will do more for your physical and mental health than getting hooked and strung out on a screen for hours.</p> <p>So it’s here, in the obsession over the release of GTA 6, that as the leader of an outdoor-related title — and within the context of an era of critically dangerous social disconnection and physical isolation — I find myself confused about why the larger culture is celebrating the creation of something that will drain our attention and time like a dystopian plug in the back of a Matrix battery-person’s skull. I just can’t celebrate.</p> <p>On one hand, a video game centered on crime and violence is probably more of a sign of the times than a cause for rising crime rates in real life. I’ll succumb to a little bit of Qoheleth-level despair recognizing here that “what has been done is what will be done, and there is nothing new under the sun.”</p> <p>And of course this is an outdoor sports publication, not Morality Weekly, but I still can’t shake another quote from the bestselling book of all time: “The eye is the lamp of the body. So, if your eye is healthy, your whole body will be full of light, but if your eye is bad, your whole body will be full of darkness.”</p> <p>GTA 6’s content notwithstanding, from my position in outdoor/endurance sports, I certainly can’t quite abide when our culture treats the release of a video game like a high holy holiday. Especially when it comes at such a high cost.</p> <p>Set aside the hard price tag of a gaming console and game (let’s say less than $1k), which is actually relatively low compared to a hobby like triathlon, and keep your eyes fixed on the time cost and quality.</p> <p>Experts from IGN note that Grand Theft Auto 6 took their editors between 42 and 115 hours to complete the new release. It’s probably a good guess that the average gamer would spend 80ish hours to noodle around in that virtual environment. That’s a lot of “life spend.”</p> <p>And unlike many other video games, it’s time spent, well…alone.</p> <p>GTA 6 has no multiplayer mode as of this writing (though it could come). This means there’s no opportunity to connect with other people — even remotely — during gameplay. You can’t commit crimes alongside your buddies in the living room; you can’t fire at police alongside friends in Sweden, Hong Kong, or France. You’re alone, for 60 hours, stealing cars and shooting people. You interact only with that world and the characters coded into it.</p> <p>Now I won’t be entirely obtuse here — not everyone can physically train for a triathlon; not everyone has time or safe access to a place where they can spend 65-150 hours outdoors, even if they wanted to.</p> <p>However, there is a large proportion of people whose time spent playing a video game (alone) will prevent them from being outside (with others) — moving their bodies through nature in some way, shape, or form.</p> <p>That’s why, rather than relish the empty streets and bike paths that have been reported across major metropolitan areas this week, I’ll be hoping that once the game ends and the credits roll (do video games have credits?), folks will head back outside to get a refund for time better spent.</p> <p style="margin-top: 25px;"> <a href="https://www.triathlete.com/culture/the-real-grand-theft/" target="_blank" rel="nofollow noopener" style="color: #0d6efd; text-decoration: none; font-weight: bold;"> → Baca artikel asli </a> </p> </div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> </div> </article> <article class="article-card" id="artikel-5225"> <script type="application/ld+json">{"@context": "https://schema.org", "@type": "NewsArticle", "headline": "Long-term memory for an AI companion without a vector database: 40 messages verbatim + 15 distilled facts", "image": ["https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F6tvrbguar1kbrz2rmht0.png"], "datePublished": "2026-11-20T22:55:10", "dateModified": "2026-11-20T22:55:10", "mainEntityOfPage": "https://railfansday.github.io/berita1jam/technology.html#artikel-5225"}</script> <h2>Long-term memory for an AI companion without a vector database: 40 messages verbatim + 15 distilled facts</h2> <div class="article-meta"> 📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: <strong>Technology</strong>  |  <a href="https://dev.to/lovora/long-term-memory-for-an-ai-companion-without-a-vector-database-40-messages-verbatim-15-distilled-11gf" target="_blank" rel="nofollow">Read Source →</a> </div> <div class="article-body"> <img src="https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F6tvrbguar1kbrz2rmht0.png" alt="Long-term memory for an AI companion without a vector database: 40 messages verbatim + 15 distilled facts" loading="lazy"> <div style="text-align:center; margin-bottom:20px;"><img src="https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F6tvrbguar1kbrz2rmht0.png" alt="Long-term memory for an AI companion without a vector database: 40 messages verbatim + 15 distilled facts" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy"></div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div class="article-body" style="font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.8; color: #333;"> <p>I run Lovora, an AI companion service for adults in nine languages. The number one thing people ask an AI companion is "will she remember me?", and "memory" turns out to mean two different things. Here is the design we use, and why.</p> <p>1. The recent conversation, word for word.</p> <p>On every reply the model receives the last 40 messages of the chat, both sides, exactly as written. In voice calls it is the last 24, because spoken turns need to be answered faster and the prompt has to stay small.</p> <p>This layer is what keeps a scene or an argument coherent. A summary would lose the exact wording, the jokes and the thing she promised two messages ago, which is exactly what people notice in a conversation.</p> <p>Every 5 messages the user sends, a separate pass reads the latest part of the conversation and updates a short list of lasting facts: at most 15 short facts, about 1,500 characters. That list goes into the prompt of every later reply, in chat and in calls.</p> <p>That is the layer that lets her ask about the job interview you mentioned last week, hundreds of messages later.</p> <p>The distillation pass is instructed to keep facts about the user, never about the character, whose personality and backstory are fixed:</p> <p>Small talk is discarded. A fact that is contradicted later is updated or dropped, not appended: the list is rewritten, not grown. The character is told to use what she remembers naturally, by asking about it or referring to it, and never to recite it as a list. Reciting is the fastest way to make a companion feel like a database.</p> <p>We chose not to retrieve past messages by similarity. For a companion it has three problems:</p> <p>A small fixed budget forces the distillation step to decide what matters, which is exactly the judgment retrieval skips.</p> <p>The verbatim window is the expensive part: 40 messages is a real share of the context on every reply. The fact pass is cheap: it runs once every 5 user messages, as a separate call on a short excerpt.</p> <p>If you are building anything conversational (support bots, tutors, companions), I would start here: a verbatim window sized to your latency budget, plus a small, rewritten list of facts about the user. Retrieval can come later if you ever need it.</p> <p>The full, current description is in our docs: How memory works on Lovora.</p> <p>DEV Community — A space to discuss and keep up software development and manage your software career</p> <p>Built on Forem — the open source software that powers DEV and other inclusive communities.</p> <p>Made with love and Ruby on Rails. DEV Community © 2016 - 2026.</p> <p>We're a place where coders share, stay up-to-date and grow their careers.</p> <p style="margin-top: 25px;"> <a href="https://dev.to/lovora/long-term-memory-for-an-ai-companion-without-a-vector-database-40-messages-verbatim-15-distilled-11gf" target="_blank" rel="nofollow noopener" style="color: #0d6efd; text-decoration: none; font-weight: bold;"> → Baca artikel asli </a> </p> </div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> </div> </article> <article class="article-card" id="artikel-5214"> <script type="application/ld+json">{"@context": "https://schema.org", "@type": "NewsArticle", "headline": "McGraw Hill, Inc. margin-bottom:20px;"><img src="https://storage.ghost.io/c/51/f8/51f871d8-b6be-4a73-b958-0ca4fff0110a/content/images/size/w1200/2026/11/_DSC3155-1.jpg" alt="In a Midtown Office, Artists Sift Through the Junkspace of Capitalism" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy"></div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div class="article-body" style="font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.8; color: #333;"> <p>An intergenerational group of artists confronts technology as both a pathogen and a cure for today’s cultural ailments in the husk of an office at 260 Madison.</p> <p>A few hours before 260 Madison opened on September 24, I trekked to its namesake address in Midtown to meet the curators. A security guard directed me to the 14th floor of the 1950s office building, where drop ceilings, an exposed HVAC system, and two people I assumed were the exhibition organizers dressed in corporate drag greeted me. They quickly clarified they were not; they were from AmTrustRE, the real estate company that offered the derelict 23,000-square-foot office space to the artists for free while preparing it — and three other unoccupied floors — for their next corporate tenants.</p> <p>The three artists-turned-curators, Shaina Tabak, Dayana Matesheva, and Edison Niebla Rodríguez (the latter two of whom make up the duo DemonLovers Inc.), organized 260 Madison as an intergenerational, “inter-everything” show with work by over 50 artists, including self-taught and non-traditionally educated practitioners. On view through this Sunday, November 22, the show arrives just as the Whitney Museum of American Art opens artport: A History of Internet Art, signaling a new wave of institutional recognition for internet-grown art. With 260 Madison artists Maya Man, Rachel Rossin, and Eva and Franco Mattes among those included in the Whitney’s 25th-anniversary exhibition for its net art commissioning platform, the Midtown show feels especially urgent, if only because it takes place at the precipice of a paradigmatic shift within the art world.</p> <p>Upon entering the first room on the left, I was immediately met with artist Jon Kessler’s towering nine-foot-tall sculpture, in which four plastic male dolls in Adidas tracksuits dangled from a weighted contraption somewhere between a carnival ride and a torture device. Titled “It Takes a Global Village Idiot” (2018) after philosopher Marshall McLuhan’s early ’60s theory that information-age technology “recreates the world in the image of a global village,” the sculpture forces viewers to confront the fact that the quick fixes of amusement proffered by technology rely on the same methods of data extraction that undergird systemic violence. It’s an unsettling introduction to a show where, even in its more humorous, delusionally conspiratorial, or enchanting moments, discomfort is always in the periphery.</p> <p>“We don’t want to make a show or art that tries to be some form of escapism,” Rogríguez told Hyperallergic during an interview the week after the opening. “It’s like the [Werner Herzog] quote, ‘The poet must not avert his eyes.’ If some art in the show seems depressing, it’s because we live in what seems a depressing time.”</p> <p>Rodríguez and Matasheva, who are also a couple, began making work together seven years ago while studying film in Montreal. Their video-art duo is named after Olivier Assayas’s 2002 film Demonlover, where multiple global corporations compete for a new immersive Japanese hentai company through extreme methods of espionage, exploitation, and blackmail.</p> <p>“DemonLovers is about the libidinal economy and how algorithms are weaponizing our very basic instincts and desires,” Matasheva told Hyperallergic, echoing Rodríguez’s interest in what he calls “financial Freud,” or the psychoanalytic dimension of digital capital flows.</p> <p>Both DemonLovers’s own two works in the show and those they curated certainly don’t shy away from the degradation associated with extractive economies, be it of the self, physical infrastructure, or the proverbial global village. Nearby Kessler’s work, Theresa Tomi Faison’s “The Long Tail” (2026) climbs up one of the floor’s load-bearing columns. Across the curved sculpture’s six screens, images of memes, forum screenshots, and phrases ruminating on contemporary capital flow all accumulate and dissolve downstream. The river of digital spam ends at the viewer’s feet, inculcating the audience, but also the space itself, which the curators characterize as shaped by "junkspace,” which architect Rem Koolhaas describes as the fallout that “remains after modernization has run its course.”</p> <p>Among works that are either readymades or play with the form’s history — including Anthony’s Discenza’s windowsill pile of unopened credit card and loan offer letters in “Offers” (2026) and Eva and Franco Mattes’s distended taxidermied cat, “Longcat” (2026) — the corporate detritus can be difficult to parse from the artistic interventions. At one point, I mistook a few square feet of wallpaper with the Champion logo for a new work. The curators clarified during a later conversation that it was actually kept from the office’s previous tenants, HanesBrands, which owns Champion.</p> <p>Having also inherited the space’s trove of gutted bathrooms, broken drywall, and stripped concrete floors (in short, its junkspace), the curators said they view 260 Madison through “layers of site responsiveness” more than anything. With some works, that connection emerged during the eleventh hour of installation. With others, the site occasioned the work itself. Ivana Dama used exposed air vents for “The Air Was Already Here” (2026), a sound work that channels air through reeds, transforming the invisible, inaudible rhythms of the space into what the artist describes as a “sculptural presence.” Pictures Generation artist Matt Mullican took a 47-foot span of unused wall space as an opportunity to reflect on his five-decade practice of constructing, deconstructing, and classifying systems of representation and perception with largely nonverbal symbols.</p> <p>“He spent three days here drawing his entire practice on the wall, right from the beginning to the end,” Matasheva said regarding Mullicant’s monumental graphite piece, “Representing The Work” (2026). “He said he will give a lecture about it and that it's a very important work for him. It's kind of a conclusion of his practice.”</p> <p>Meanwhile, the show’s video works are lodged into broken columns, ceiling alcoves, liminal backrooms, bathroom plumbing, abstract paintings, and even a hanging pink punching bag, a veritable reunion of the last decade of post-internet art movements. Alumni from Joshua Citarella’s publishing platform Do Not Research, the New Museum-affiliated Rhizome, and the traveling video and film screening series Open Secret, including Faison, Dana Dawud, Yehwan Song, and Ruba Al-Sweel, have contributed works. Many of them feature themes of surveillance, internet-induced conspiracy, and social media performance, as well as the technological, often AI-abetted methods of production that have come to define such enclaves.</p> <p>Yet, for all the immateriality and speculative knowledge that flows through these works, 260 Madison equally plumbs an idea of technology with a long, craft-driven history. Tabak, a sculptor with a background in furniture design, is the driving force behind this facet of the show — and the currents of divination, enchantment, and even sublimity that come with it.</p> <p>“My practice is very handmade and material-focused, so I always know that that’s what I would contribute to the curatorial vision,” Tabak told Hyperallergic. “And just being in Midtown, you shouldn’t separate a show from what’s happening around it.</p> <p>Tabak was particularly interested in curating works that “blur the line between the hand and technology,” inviting artists who “haven’t had a lot of chances to show in art contexts, whether it’s because they are in the design world or have nine-to-fives” to participate in the exhibition. She explains that many of them complicate the very notion of technology and its use, citing Brian Oakes’s “Vessel 9” (2025), which gathers 3D-modeled circuit boards into a suspended “techno-Gothic” chandelier, and Ryan Decker’s “The Wavemaker Falters” (2026), which uses a motorized, rudder-like instrument to swoosh water through a raised vessel resembling a spaceship.</p> <p>“We view technology not as something that has arrived in the 20th century, but something that has made humanity possible,” Matasheva said, expanding on Tabak’s comment. “Language is a technology, a chisel is a technology, everything is a technology.”</p> <p>While many of the show’s craft-driven works conjure the anthropologist Alfred Gell’s idea that art is a technology that enchants, and in turn, becomes enchanted, others wield technology in the service of care. Kylie Mitchell repurposes a light-seeking solar robot and discarded electronic parts to move a found bird’s nest and egg toward a life-sustaining heat lamp. A few dozen feet away, Gregory Kalliche’s installation “Black-tailed gull nest; Ring-necked Pheasant Nest; Mallard Nest” (2016) suspends an LED light over three metal folding chairs printed with images of bird egg nests. Ben Gould’s “Ark II” (2026), on the other hand, augments a hospital gurney with eight aluminum armatures that hold fresh red roses inside coil-shaped glass vials. Doubling as a literal manifestation of Shakespeare’s “this mortal coil” phrase in Hamlet, the sculpture’s sterile infrastructure butts against the transitory visual comfort proffered by its flowers, mirroring a healthcare ecosystem wherein privatization, optimization, and tech automation have whittled medicine’s promises of care, healing, and empathy into mere aesthetic veneers.</p> <p>It’s no coincidence that many of the works in 260 Madison position technology as both a pathogen and cure for today’s cultural ailments. In fact, when the real estate developers initially contacted Columbia University’s School of the Arts, it was to invite artists to “activate” a 36,000-square-foot gutted Duane Reade on the building’s ground floor. The developers eventually retracted their offer and moved the exhibition to the 14th floor, citing ongoing construction. Meanwhile, the original plan for 260 Madison to occupy the husk of the Duane Reade will be realized in a different form next month. Now serving as the building’s new temporary lobby, the former pharmacy will play host to a different group exhibition curated independently by Tabak. Opening December 3, HOW I FEEL will feature artists working across sculpture, social architecture, photography, painting, performance, and programming, including Jessica Vaughn, Quay Quinn Wolf, André Magaña, and 260 Madison’s Grayson Cox and Luke Murphy. How the forthcoming Duane Reade show contends with this moment of tech-induced economic entropy, so intense that even an entity as extractive as a real-estate company cannot commit to its own speculations, remains to be seen.</p> <p>NYSS is now accepting applications from independent thinkers and experimenters in the arts.</p> <p>The foundation invites applications from mission-aligned arts and science organizations for grants of up to $20,000 through March 31, 2027.</p> <p>A list of arts-related graduate programs to explore and apply to before deadlines close.</p> <p>Co-developed by National Gallery Singapore and independent curator Alia Swastika, the course foregrounds women’s contributions to Indonesian art.</p> <p>Get the best of Hyperallergic sent straight to your inbox.</p> <p style="margin-top: 25px;"> <a href="https://hyperallergic.com/in-a-midtown-office-artists-sift-through-the-junkspace-of-capitalism/" target="_blank" rel="nofollow noopener" style="color: #0d6efd; text-decoration: none; font-weight: bold;"> → Baca artikel asli </a> </p> </div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> </div> </article> <article class="article-card" id="artikel-5227"> <script type="application/ld+json">{"@context": "https://schema.org", "@type": "NewsArticle", "headline": "How to Run Long-Running AI Agents in Production (2026): Runtime Limits, Durable State, and Surviving Restarts and Outages", "image": ["https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F5wf4ozpl3idb1qyc0yh1.png"], "datePublished": "2026-11-20T22:51:03", "dateModified": "2026-11-20T22:51:03", "mainEntityOfPage": "https://railfansday.github.io/berita1jam/technology.html#artikel-5227"}</script> <h2>How to Run Long-Running AI Agents in Production (2026): Runtime Limits, Durable State, and Surviving Restarts and Outages</h2> <div class="article-meta"> 📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: <strong>Technology</strong>  |  <a href="https://dev.to/noahcontrolplane/how-to-run-long-running-ai-agents-in-production-2026-runtime-limits-durable-state-and-ahp" target="_blank" rel="nofollow">Read Source →</a> </div> <div class="article-body"> <img src="https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F5wf4ozpl3idb1qyc0yh1.png" alt="How to Run Long-Running AI Agents in Production (2026): Runtime Limits, Durable State, and Surviving Restarts and Outages" loading="lazy"> <div style="text-align:center; margin-bottom:20px;"><img src="https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F5wf4ozpl3idb1qyc0yh1.png" alt="How to Run Long-Running AI Agents in Production (2026): Runtime Limits, Durable State, and Surviving Restarts and Outages" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy"></div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div class="article-body" style="font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.8; color: #333;"> <p>Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at controlplane.com • AI-assisted</p> <p>Long-running AI agents need three things that serverless request handlers do not give them: a process with no short execution cap, state checkpointed outside the process so a restart resumes from the last step, and presence in more than one location so a provider outage does not stop the agent. Most teams find these gaps after a prototype works in development. In production it dies mid-run when a function times out at 15 minutes, a deploy kills the container, or a zone goes offline.</p> <p>Disclosure: Control Plane publishes this guide.</p> <p>Long-running AI agents fail in production when the infrastructure treats them like short-lived request handlers.</p> <p>Production agents need a runtime that does not stop them after a few minutes, a state layer that survives process death, and enough redundancy to stay up through infrastructure failures.</p> <p>Execution limits vary widely, and hitting a cap mid-run loses work unless a durable layer sits on top.</p> <p>Short-lived serverless functions need a durable execution layer on top, and long-running container runtimes still need graceful shutdown and external state.</p> <p>The right state layer depends on your existing infrastructure, how complex the workflow is, and how long the agent needs to pause.</p> <p>A team that already runs Postgres can add durability with DBOS or the LangGraph Postgres checkpointer and little new infrastructure. Complex multi-day orchestration with many branches fits Temporal. The OpenAI Agents SDK documents integrations with Temporal, Restate, DBOS, and Dapr for long-running agents, and the OpenAI Responses API background mode runs long requests that are polled or delivered by webhook. A single cloud’s durable service keeps agent state inside that provider’s failure domain, so a provider outage stops the workflow along with the agent.</p> <p>Eleven failure modes regularly stop production agents, and each has a control that prevents lost work.</p> <p>Every control depends on state living outside the process and outside any single failure domain.</p> <p>Graceful shutdown, rolling updates, multi-location replicas, and checkpoint resume work together.</p> <p>When a deploy, scale-in, or node drain happens, the orchestrator sends SIGTERM to the container. The agent should catch it, stop picking up new steps, and complete or checkpoint the current step. A preStop hook runs before SIGTERM, and its time counts against the termination grace period, which defaults to 30 seconds on Kubernetes.</p> <p>Rolling updates keep capacity during deploys by replacing replicas in batches (on Kubernetes, by default no more than 25% unavailable at once) and waiting for each new replica to pass readiness probes. A PodDisruptionBudget limits how many replicas are down at once during voluntary disruptions such as node drains; it does not throttle a Deployment’s own rolling update.</p> <p>Multi-location replicas protect against zone, region, and provider outages. If the agent runs in two locations and one fails, the other keeps serving. Checkpointed state must live in a store the surviving replicas can reach, such as a Postgres database replicated across regions. Asynchronous replication can lose the latest checkpoints on failover, so the resumed step must be safe to repeat, and leases belong on one strongly consistent primary at a time. When that primary’s location fails, promote a replica in a healthy location and advance the fencing epoch so writes from the old primary’s era are rejected.</p> <p>Resuming from checkpoints ties these together. A replica in a healthy location loads the last checkpoint and continues. Steps whose completion was recorded are skipped, the interrupted step runs again, and idempotency keys let that retry run without duplicate side effects. Routing users to the nearest healthy region covers the traffic side.</p> <p>Control Plane handles Day 2 operations for you, including autoscaling, built-in observability, and security.</p> <p>It runs five workload types: serverless, standard, stateful, cron, and VM. On Control Plane, standard workloads are long-running processes, so an agent runs continuously with no execution cap. Stateful workloads give each replica a stable identity and its own persistent volume; a replica keeps the same identity (such as agent-0) when it is restarted or rescheduled, each replica is bound to its own volume from a volume set, and volume sets support snapshots. An inbound HTTP request times out after at most 600 seconds, or up to 3,600 seconds with the cpln/timeoutSecondsOverride workload tag, so run longer agent tasks as background work in the workload, with clients polling for status or receiving a callback.</p> <p>A Global Virtual Cloud (GVC) is Control Plane’s named set of locations across AWS, GCP, Azure, and Oracle Cloud Infrastructure regions, plus your own Kubernetes clusters. A workload runs in every location of its GVC. Each workload’s endpoint routes clients to the nearest location where it passes readiness probes, so when one location fails, traffic reaches the healthy ones. In-flight work in the failed location resumes elsewhere only if its state was checkpointed to a store the other location can reach and its lease has expired; the fencing token on checkpoint writes keeps a cut-off replica from committing progress for the same run, and idempotency keys keep its side effects from repeating.</p> <p>For that store, Control Plane’s Template Catalog includes PostgreSQL, PostgreSQL HA, and multi-location databases such as Postgres Multi-Location and pgEdge, which can back DBOS, the LangGraph Postgres checkpointer, or a custom checkpoint table; keep lease and fencing writes on a single primary, because multi-master replication resolves conflicts after the write. Temporal is also in the Template Catalog for teams that orchestrate with it. By default each replica gets a 90-second termination grace period, configurable in the workload’s rollout options. When a replica shuts down, Control Plane first removes it from load balancing, a default preStop hook then waits half the grace period (45 seconds at the default), and the container then receives SIGTERM, leaving the rest of the grace period for the agent to checkpoint. A queue-driven agent should also stop claiming runs when the preStop hook starts, since removal from load balancing only stops new HTTP requests.</p> <p>Control Plane’s Universal Cloud Identity lets workloads call AWS, GCP, and Azure services with no long-lived cloud keys in your code. Control Plane Sandboxes run in Kata Containers microVMs and are persistent; they are for agents that build and run untrusted code, while the production agent runs as a workload. The Control Plane MCP server lets an agent operate infrastructure through the same Control Plane API as the CLI and console. Control Plane is PCI DSS Level 1, SOC 2 Type II, HIPAA, and GDPR compliant.</p> <p>During the October 20, 2025 AWS us-east-1 outage, 20% of Control Plane customers had AWS us-east-1 in their GVC. Their workloads were active and running in healthy locations within 10 seconds, with no customer downtime, according to Control Plane’s internal incident telemetry.</p> <p>Related reading: where to run AI agents in production, 10 AI agent infrastructure platforms compared, the cost to run AI agents in production, and letting AI agents operate production infrastructure safely.</p> <p>These answers cover the questions engineering teams ask when they move AI agents from prototype to production.</p> <p>Run them as long-lived processes on a runtime with no execution cap, checkpoint state to a durable store after every meaningful step, make side-effecting tool calls idempotent, handle SIGTERM during deploys and scale-in, retry model and tool errors with backoff, and run replicas in two or more locations.</p> <p>Only with a durable layer on top, because a single Lambda invocation stops at 15 minutes. AWS Lambda durable functions extend runs to up to 1 year by checkpointing progress and replaying after failures, and AWS Step Functions Standard workflows also run up to 1 year.</p> <p>Checkpoint the agent’s state to an external durable store after each completed step, then load the last checkpoint when the process restarts. Steps recorded as complete are skipped, and idempotency keys let the interrupted step run again without duplicate effects.</p> <p>Durable execution persists a workflow’s progress so that after a crash, restart, or deploy, it resumes from the last completed step. Temporal rebuilds state by replaying event history, Inngest treats each step as a checkpointed unit of work, and DBOS checkpoints workflows and steps to Postgres.</p> <p>In a durable external store that survives restarts and that every location running the agent can reach. Postgres is common, used directly by DBOS and the LangGraph Postgres checkpointer, while Temporal keeps state in its own persistence layer.</p> <p>They run replicas in locations on more than one cloud provider and keep checkpointed state in a store reachable from all of them. When one provider fails, traffic moves to healthy locations, and a replica there claims the run, loads the last checkpoint, and continues. On Control Plane, a Global Virtual Cloud spanning AWS, GCP, Azure, and Oracle Cloud Infrastructure locations routes clients to healthy locations automatically.</p> <p>No. Temporal suits complex multi-day orchestration, and Inngest, Restate, DBOS, AWS Step Functions, Azure Durable Functions, and Cloudflare Workflows also provide durable execution with different trade-offs. A simple workflow can also use custom checkpoint logic on Postgres.</p> <p>As standard or stateful workloads with no execution cap, running in every location of a Global Virtual Cloud across AWS, GCP, Azure, and Oracle Cloud Infrastructure. Each workload’s endpoint routes to the nearest location that passes readiness probes, stateful replicas keep persistent volumes with snapshots, and a 90-second default grace period, configurable per workload, lets in-flight work drain and gives the agent time after SIGTERM to checkpoint.</p> <p>Agent memory is the context an agent reasons with, such as conversation history and preferences, while durable execution state records which steps finished, what they returned, and what runs next. Memory can be summarized over time; execution state must be exact so a crashed agent resumes without repeating or losing work.</p> <p>A rolling deploy replaces replicas in batches, and each old replica gets SIGTERM and a limited grace period. An agent that checkpoints on SIGTERM and releases its run continues on another replica from the last checkpoint; one that ignores SIGTERM loses its in-flight work.</p> <p>Talk to the Control Plane team to run long-running AI agents across clouds, or sign up free to start building.</p> <p>Originally published on the Control Plane blog.</p> <p>DEV Community — A space to discuss and keep up software development and manage your software career</p> <p>Built on Forem — the open source software that powers DEV and other inclusive communities.</p> <p>Made with love and Ruby on Rails. </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> </div> </article> <article class="article-card" id="artikel-5157"> <script type="application/ld+json">{"@context": "https://schema.org", "@type": "NewsArticle", "headline": "Bitcoin miners face a new profitability cliff as difficulty heads for an 8.5% jump", "image": ["https://cryptoslate.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/bitcoin-miner-power-margin.webp"], "datePublished": "2026-11-20T22:50:55+00:00", "dateModified": "2026-11-20T22:50:55+00:00", "mainEntityOfPage": "https://railfansday.github.io/berita1jam/technology.html#artikel-5157"}</script> <h2>Bitcoin miners face a new profitability cliff as difficulty heads for an 8.5% jump</h2> <div class="article-meta"> 📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: <strong>Technology</strong>  |  <a href="https://cryptoslate.com/bitcoin-miners-face-shrinking-revenue-and-tighter-electricity-margins-as-a-projected-difficulty-increase-threatens-less-efficient-machines/" target="_blank" rel="nofollow">Read Source →</a> </div> <div class="article-body"> <img src="https://cryptoslate.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/bitcoin-miner-power-margin.webp" alt="Bitcoin miners face a new profitability cliff as difficulty heads for an 8.5% jump" loading="lazy"> <div style="text-align:center; margin-bottom:20px;"><img src="https://cryptoslate.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/bitcoin-miner-power-margin.webp" alt="Bitcoin miners face a new profitability cliff as difficulty heads for an 8.5% jump" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy"></div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div class="article-body" style="font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.8; color: #333;"> <p>Bitcoin miners face shrinking revenue and tighter electricity margins as a projected difficulty increase threatens less efficient machines.</p> <p>Bitcoin miners’ expected dollar revenue for the same computing power fell about 6% from Nov. 16 to Nov. 20 in two readings of Luxor’s revenue index.</p> <p>A difficulty cut had lifted expected Bitcoin earnings earlier in the month, but with the next adjustment now tracking higher, less efficient machines could soon need cheaper electricity to cover their power bills.</p> <p>Simple Mining’s Nov. 20 reading of Luxor’s hash price index put gross mining revenue at $28.58 per petahash per second (PH/s) per day, with Luxor’s Nov. 16 roundup listing $30.45.</p> <p>To estimate a machine’s gross daily revenue, multiply the hash price by the machine’s computing power in PH/s. One PH/s equals 1,000 terahashes per second.</p> <p>The slide also appeared in CloudMineCrypto’s daily series, which estimates revenue from Bitcoin blocks. In its partial Nov. 20 reading, dollar hash price had fallen to about $28.90 from $30.30 per PH/s per day on Nov. 16, while the coin rate held near 0.00047 BTC.</p> <p>The paired readings explain why a miner can earn roughly the same amount of Bitcoin for a day of work and still have fewer dollars to pay an electricity bill.</p> <p>Bitcoin’s Nov. 14 adjustment lowered difficulty by 9.13%, from 148.36 trillion to 134.82 trillion. Holding the reward per block steady, the same amount of work could then earn about 10% more Bitcoin. Luxor’s Nov. 16 roundup found that the BTC hash price had risen from 0.00042763 a week earlier to 0.00046839 BTC per PH/s per day.</p> <p>Over the same period, the dollar hash price fell from $31.95 to $30.45.</p> <p>The expected coin payout depends on the block subsidy, transaction fees, and how hard it is to find a block. The dollar payout also depends on Bitcoin’s price, since fees accounted for only a small fraction of block rewards in both data sets.</p> <p>Luxor reported a weekly average of 0.0229 BTC in fees per block on Nov. 16, and the partial Nov. 20 data imply about 0.022 BTC in fees on top of the 3.125 BTC subsidy.</p> <p>As of Nov. 20, MineOrNot counted 1,008 blocks, halfway to the next adjustment. Blocks were arriving about every nine minutes and 14 seconds, compared with Bitcoin’s ten-minute target.</p> <p>If that pace persists, MineOrNot estimated difficulty would rise 8.47% to about 146.24 trillion around Nov. 27.</p> <p>If Bitcoin’s price, the subsidy, and average fees per block stay fixed, an 8.47% rise in difficulty would lower the $28.58 benchmark to about $26.35 per PH/s per day. A 10% increase would put it near $25.98 and bring difficulty almost back to its pre-cut level.</p> <p>For a machine using 30 joules per terahash, the observed benchmark translates to a power-only break-even rate of 3.97 cents per kilowatt-hour.</p> <p>With an 8.47% increase in difficulty, the same machine’s ceiling would fall to 3.66 cents. A machine using 13.5 joules per terahash can absorb higher electricity rates, with its ceiling falling from 8.82 cents to 8.13 cents under the same assumptions.</p> <p>Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.</p> <p>Gross mining revenue can cover a lower electricity rate if difficulty rises 8.47%, even with Bitcoin’s price and block rewards held fixed.</p> <p>At a hypothetical 3.8¢/kWh, a 30 J/TH rig would stop covering its power bill if difficulty rises 8.47%. The 13.5 J/TH rig would still cover electricity.</p> <p>Electricity-only model. Excludes pool fees, hosting, hardware and other costs.</p> <p>Use the November 20 benchmark of $28.58 per PH/s per day. Model the retarget by dividing by 1.0847. At 1 PH/s, the 30 and 13.5 J/TH rigs draw 30 and 13.5 kW. Divide daily gross revenue by 24 times power in kW, multiply by 100, and round to 0.01 cent per kWh.</p> <p>Electricity-only model. Excludes pool fees, hosting, hardware and other costs.</p> <p>At a hypothetical electricity rate of 3.8 cents per kWh, the less efficient machine would just cover its power bill at the Nov. 20 benchmark. At that same rate, it would fall short after an 8.47% increase in difficulty. The more efficient machine would cover electricity at that rate in both cases.</p> <p>The calculation divides gross daily revenue by daily electricity use. A miner at the ceiling has already used all its expected revenue on electricity. Pool fees and any separate hosting charges can lower the rate it can afford, and the operator’s contract and downtime will shape its actual costs.</p> <p>Also known as "Akiba," Liam Wright is a reporter, podcast producer, and Editor-in-Chief at CryptoSlate. </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div class="article-body" style="font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.8; color: #333;"> <p>It posted an encouraging third-quarter earnings report.</p> <p>By Eric Volkman – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:48PM EST</p> <p>Globant (GLOB +4.13%) was one of the rare stocks to rise amid the sharp decline of the wider market on Friday. It acquired that port-in-a-storm quality on its latest set of quarterly results, which were good enough to draw investors to its equity. The digital transformation specialist's shares ended the day more than 4% higher.</p> <p>Globant's third-quarter revenue was $611.1 million, down 1% year over year. Non-IFRS (adjusted) net income also declined, falling by almost 9% to nearly $63.8 million, or $1.48 per share.</p> <p>That earnings per share (EPS) figure met analyst expectations while revenue notched a slight beat. The pundit top-line consensus for that line item was $610.9 million.</p> <p>Like the more progressive legacy software companies, Globant -- which specializes in developing custom software for enterprises automating aspects of their business -- has embraced artificial intelligence (AI) in its solutions. It said in its earnings release that its recently introduced AI Pods subscription service had doubled its annualized run rate.</p> <p>Globant quoted co-founder and CEO Martin Migoya as saying that "We are seeing growing enterprise adoption as clients shift from hourly billing toward outcome- and consumption-based delivery."</p> <p>It's one thing to embrace AI as either a tool for internal software development or to tack on some of its functionalities to an existing software suite. It's quite another to lead with it as a product offering, like Globant is doing with AI Pods.</p> <p>The service is clearly resonating with the market, and I think it might soon become a driver of growth for this company given its relatively high margins. This is starting to look something like a sleeper stock among the many software titles on the market, and as such, it's well worth watching.</p> <p>When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. While we strive for accuracy and editorial quality, readers should independently verify important information.</p> <p>Two goals inside 2 minutes settled Friday night’s round 11 match at An der Alten Försterei early. By the 11th minute Leverkusen were in control, and Valentina Mädl added a third on the hour to give Union Berlin a long evening on 20 November.</p> <p>Sternad opened the scoring in the 9th minute, finishing a move set up by Cornelia Kramer. Her pass found Marlene Müller, who doubled the lead.</p> <p>Sternad was rated 8.53, the highest on the pitch, and created 2 chances in total across her 90 minutes. It does not mean you understand it.</p> <p>Levent Alpöge says he and Claude formalized a proof of the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, without assumptions. He also says he has not mastered the mathematics sitting around that argument.</p> <p>He says the formalization is finished. Turning it into a huge document with sufficient detail is, in his account, routine. He amused himself and got past 250,000 pages. Claude called the pile a book.</p> <p>Theorem 1 opens by noting that the real numbers are totally ordered. If a number is not one half, it is bigger, or it is smaller. A checker will spend a page saying so. That is how you know a human never edited it for a reader.</p> <p>He did not post the pile. He says posting that unreadable dump would be inappropriate. He published the Lean instead, in fourteen parts.</p> <p>He is showing a complete argument before he has mastered a large neighborhood of it. The files are ahead of the author.</p> <p>Where Claude did the technical work, his timeline is a paraphrase of a summary he warns may make no sense. He points at the Lean. He says he is too inexpert there to explain it himself.</p> <p>His instinct is that the argument will get much simpler, and soon. He has not had time to find that shorter version. He calls this one step. The unpaid work is to understand it, simplify it, talk about it, and write it properly.</p> <p>Same trap as a generated patch you cannot walk someone through. The green run is not the tour.</p> <p>The Lean can check out while you are still late to your own result.</p> <p>A green build is permission to start explaining. While there was plenty of debate over whether the critic reviews and player responses would actually line up or not when it released, the public response has been almost equally impressive so far.</p> <p>Despite taking the industry by storm and boasting sales that broke a new record for the entertainment industry, GTA 6's review rating has just plummeted on Metacritic immediately after the site opened its user scores.</p> <p>As noted by users like CharlieIntel on X, while there are plenty of user reviews that note the flaws while celebrating its achievements, the User Review section for GTA 6 on Metacritic has been absolutely flooded with overwhelmingly negative reviews, many of which are from players who admit they "haven't even played the game" themselves.</p> <p>With GTA 6 currently sitting at a 5.1 user score at the time of writing, over half of the reviews uploaded for the game are negative, with countless politically charged reviews calling it "woke slop" and a "cuck margin-bottom:20px;"><img src="https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2Fr93h34uaeppolug1rihg.png" alt="How to fix "MCP: Unsupported protocol version"" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy"></div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div class="article-body" style="font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.8; color: #333;"> <p>Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at runheed.com</p> <p>Returned with HTTP 400 (code -32000) by HTTP MCP servers built on @modelcontextprotocol/sdk 1.x when a request's MCP-Protocol-Version header names a version the SDK does not know. SDK 1.32.2, the last 1.x release, supports 2025-11-25, 2025-06-18, 2025-03-26, 2024-11-05 and 2024-10-07. MCP 2026-07-28 is the first stateless revision: it drops the initialize handshake, so a client speaking it sends the version on every request, and the Python SDK 2.x lists it as its latest version.</p> <p>If clients still connect, there is nothing to fix today: a client that supports both eras treats a 400 without a modern error body as a legacy server and falls back to initialize, which your 1.x server answers. To serve clients that speak only 2026-07-28, move the server to the v2 package (@modelcontextprotocol/server), which supports both. Do not fake the new protocol's UnsupportedProtocolVersionError (-32022) from a 1.x server: that error tells a client the server is modern, so it stops falling back.</p> <p>DEV Community — A space to discuss and keep up software development and manage your software career</p> <p>Built on Forem — the open source software that powers DEV and other inclusive communities.</p> <p>Made with love and Ruby on Rails. text-decoration: none; font-weight: bold;"> → Baca artikel asli </a> </p> </div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; Before delivery, Konstra AI handles everything from architecture design and rack deployment through tuning, then validates each cluster with tests such as NCCL and HPL, so customers receive compute that is ready for training and inference from day one. Enterprises that want to use models directly can connect to more than 70 leading models through a single key, with prepaid billing and no monthly fee, keeping AI usage and budgets manageable and traceable. Horizon AI is an enterprise AI adoption partner and a member of the NVIDIA Inception program. From GPU clusters and flexible compute to model access, enterprises can start at any layer and expand as they scale, always working with a single point of contact.</p> <p>"SC26 is where the global HPC community comes together, and we want international partners to see that they can find compute services in Asia that connect everything from GPU clusters and cloud platforms to model governance," said Ben Chang, co-founder and chairman of KONST. KONST aims to be the partner Asian enterprises and investors can rely on for AI infrastructure.</p> <p>Every engagement starts with a diagnosis: Horizon AI assesses an enterprise's AI use cases and compute needs; Konstra AI evaluates a site's power and build conditions and handles execution from architecture design and data center construction to 24/7 operations; and (k) ATP Token connects leading models through a single key, making AI usage and budgets manageable and traceable. color: #333;"> <p>Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.</p> <p>The Apple TV Store currently looks less like a digital storefront and more like Starfleet Command, thanks to the ongoing discounted sale on the store. One particular franchise seems to be taking an exceptional lead during the sale, which proves that Trekkers are still solid even 6 decades later.</p> <p>According to FlixPatrol's November 18 U.S. rankings, six of the platform's 10 most-popular television titles belong to Star Trek, while a seventh has cracked the Top 20. The sale went live around November 16 and includes titles like even Game of Thrones, Friends, The Sopranos, Supernatural, and The Big Bang Theory, among others. Most of these titles are cult favorites, but Star Trek still seems to have had a very strong breakthrough, which is what makes the dominance so impressive.</p> <p>The whole Apple TV Store covers a large slice of Star Trek. For instance, The Original Series is $20 at the time of writing, instead of the original $60 price tag. The Animated Series is at $10 instead of $30. The Next Generation is at $40 instead of $100, while Star Trek: Enterprise, Star Trek: Picard, and Star Trek: Lower Decks are at $20 from their original $50 prices. There’s also Prodigy, which is at $15 (at a 50% discount) right now. In contrast, all eight seasons of Game of Thrones are currently available at $60 (original price $100), down 40% from its original price and still not making it to the Top 20 on Apple TV Store.</p> <p>Paramount spent this year running a major anniversary campaign around six decades of the franchise, which now encompasses 11 television series, 14 files and more than 900 episodes. There were anniversary screenings, worldwide events, a Guinness World Records initiative around the Vulcan salute, merchandising collaborations and other celebrations throughout 2026. That whole advertising grind from Paramount’s end seems to have beautifully allowed the Apple TV Store to lock in on that traction.</p> <p>Other than the ones already mentioned, there’s Star Trek: Deep Space Nine available on Apple TV Store for $35, but its shallower discount may help explain its lower #16 placement. Strange New Worlds is still an ongoing series sold season-by-season, which explains why it’s not on the list.</p> <p>Star Trek is currently trending on the Apple TV Store and available at heavily discounted prices.
